Find the sum of all integer bases $b>9$ for which $17_{b}$ is a divisor of $97_{b}$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer bases b>9 such that the number "17" in base b is a divisor of "97" in base b. In base b, 17_b = 1*b + 7 = b + 7. 97_b = 9*b + 7. We need (b+7) divides (9b+7). So find integer b>9 with positive integer divisor. Compute: 9b+7 = 9(b+7) - 56? Actually 9(b+7) = 9b + 63. 9b+7 = 9(b+7) - 56 = 9b+63 - 56 = 9b+7 indeed. So dividing 9b+7 by b+7 gives quotient 9 minus 56/(b+7). So we require (b+7) divides 56. Actually because 9b+7 = 9(b+7) -56, thus (b+7) | 9(b+7) so it divides difference: 9(b+7) - (9b+7) = 56. Wait correct: (b+7)|(9b+7). Compute 9b+7 = 9(b+7) -56, so we have 9(b+7) -56 = 9b+7. So if (b+7)| 9b+7, then (b+7)| 9(b+7)- (9b+7) = 56. So we require b+7 divides 56.

Also, b+7 > 7+10? Wait b>9 => b+7 > 16. So we need divisors of 56 greater than 16: divisors of 56: 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. Divisors >16: 28 and 56. Also need b+7 = divisor. So possible values:

Case 1: b+7 = 28 => b = 21.

Case 2: b+7 = 56 => b = 49.

Also we need b integer >9. Both are >9. But need to also ensure that 17_b is a divisor of 97_b. Since we derived condition b+7|56 is necessary. But is it also sufficient? Since 9b+7 = 9(b+7)-56, if b+7 divides 56 then it divides 9b+7. Yes. So sufficiency holds. But check that base b must allow digits 7 and 9 (since digits in base are 0..b-1). For base b>9: digits 0-9 allowed; but 9 is allowed only if base >9? In base 10, digits 0-9. For base b>9, digits allowed 0,...,b-1 including 9. For base 49, digits up to 48: so 9 allowed. For base 21, digits up to 20: includes 9. So valid.

Thus sum of integer bases: 21 + 49 = 70. So answer 70.

Let's double-check: 17_b equal to b+7; 97_b equal to 9b+7. For b=21: 17_21 = 28; 97_21 = 9*21+7=196. 196/28=7. Good. For b=49: 17_49 = 56; 97_49 = 9*49+7=448, 448/56=8. Good.

Thus answer 70.

Sampled message

assistant 
In base \(b>9\)

\[
17_b = 1\cdot b + 7 = b+7,\qquad   
97_b = 9\cdot b + 7.
\]

We need \(b+7\) to divide \(9b+7\).

---

### Use the Euclidean algorithm

\[
9b+7 = 9(b+7)-56.
\]

If \(b+7\) divides \(9b+7\), then it must also divide the difference

\[
9(b+7) - (9b+7)= 56.
\]

Hence

\[
b+7 \mid 56.
\]

So \(b+7\) must be a divisor of 56 that is **greater than 16** (since \(b>9\) implies \(b+7>16\)).

Divisors of 56: \(1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56\).

Divisors \(>16\): \(28\) and \(56\).

---

### Find the corresponding bases

1. \(b+7 = 28 \;\Rightarrow\; b = 21.\)
2. \(b+7 = 56 \;\Rightarrow\; b = 49.\)

Both \(b=21\) and \(b=49\) satisfy \(b>9\).  
Digits \(1,7,9\) are valid in these bases (since \(b>9\)).

Check:

- For \(b=21\): \(17_{21}=28\), \(97_{21}=196\) and \(196/28=7\).
- For \(b=49\): \(17_{49}=56\), \(97_{49}=448\) and \(448/56=8\).

Thus both bases work.

---

### Sum of all such bases

\[
21 + 49 = 70.
\]

\[
\boxed{70}
\]

Results

Correct Answer: 70

Extracted Answer: 70

Reflections

### 1.  Area of quadrilateral \(DEGF\)

Using the shoelace formula for the vertices  
\(D(4,0),\;E(20,0),\;G(\tfrac{5c_x}{7},\tfrac{5c_y}{7}),\;F(\tfrac{c_x}{7},\tfrac{c_y}{7})\):

\[
\operatorname{area}(DEGF)=\frac12\Big|\,(96c_y/7)\,\Big|
     =\frac{48}{7}|c_y|.
\]

Given \(\operatorname{area}(DEGF)=288\),

\[
\frac{48}{7}|c_y|=288\quad\Longrightarrow\quad |c_y|=42 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Area of \(\triangle ABC\)

Side \(AB\) has length \(28\) and lies on the \(x\)-axis, hence

\[
\operatorname{area}(\triangle ABC)=\frac12\,(AB)\,|c_y|
  =\frac12 \cdot 28\cdot 42= 588 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Area of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\)

Apply the shoelace formula to the consecutive vertices

\[
A(0,0),\;F(\tfrac{c_x}{7},\tfrac{c_y}{7}),\;
N(40-\tfrac{5c_x}{7},-\tfrac{5c_y}{7}),\;
B(28,0),\;
C(c_x,c_y),\;
E(20,0),\;
M(\tfrac{2c_x}{7}-4,\tfrac{2c_y}{7}).
\]

The cross‑product terms simplify dramatically; after cancelling
terms containing \(\frac{c_x}{7}\), the net sum is

\[
\sum (x_i y_{i+1}-y_i x_{i+1}) = 28\,c_y .
\]

Hence

\[
\operatorname{area}(AFNBCEM)
   =\frac12\,|28\,c_y|=14|c_y|=588 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

The heptagon’s area equals that of \(\triangle ABC\).
Therefore

\[
\boxed{588}
\]

assistant

Discriminant D = b^2 - 4ac = (-y)^2 - 4*12*(-6 y^2) = y^2 + 288 y^2 = 289 y^2 = (17 y)^2 because 289 = 17^2. So sqrt(D) = 17 |y|. So x = [y ± 17 |y|]/(2*12) = [y ± 17|y|]/24. Since y integer, consider sign of y.

1) x = y(1 - 17)/24 = -16 y/24 = -2y/3.

assistant 
To solve  

\[
12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0 , \qquad x,y\in[-100,100]\cap\mathbb Z
\]

view it as a quadratic in \(x\):

\[
12x^{2}-yx-6y^{2}=0.
\]

Its discriminant is  

\[
\Delta = (-y)^{2}-4(12)(-6y^{2}) = y^{2}+288y^{2}=289y^{2}=(17|y|)^{2}.
\]

Hence  

\[
x=\frac{y\pm 17|y|}{24}.
\]

Because \(|y|\) is either \(y\) or \(-y\), both possibilities give the same two linear relations

\[
\boxed{x=\frac{3}{4}y}\qquad\text{or}\qquad \boxed{x=-\frac{2}{3}y}.
\]

---

### 1.  Solutions of \(x=\dfrac{3}{4}y\)

Let \(y=4k\) with \(k\in\mathbb Z\).  
Then \(x=3k\).  
The bounds \(|x|\le 100,\ |y|\le100\) give

\[
|3k|\le 100\quad\Rightarrow\quad |k|\le 33,\qquad
|4k|\le 100\quad\Rightarrow\quad |k|\le 25.
\]

Thus \(|k|\le 25\).  
Number of possibilities: \(k=-25,-24,\dots,0,\dots,24,25\) — **51** pairs.

---

### 2.  Solutions of \(x=-\dfrac{2}{3}y\)

Let \(y=3k\) with \(k\in\mathbb Z\).  
Then \(x=-2k\).  
The bounds give

\[
|3k|\le 100\quad\Rightarrow\quad |k|\le 33,\qquad
|2k|\le 100\quad\Rightarrow\quad |k|\le 50.
\]

So \(|k|\le 33\).  
Number of possibilities: \(k=-33,-32,\dots,0,\dots,32,33\) — **67** pairs.

---

### 3.  Removing duplication

The two families coincide only when \(k=0\), yielding the point \((0,0)\).  
Thus we subtract one duplicate.

\[
\text{Total} = 51 + 67 - 1 = 117.
\]

---

\[
\boxed{117}
\]

First, note that a required number must be even and divisible by \(11\).

*The even condition*  
The last digit must be one of \(\{2,4,6,8\}\).

*The divisibility‑by‑11 condition*  
Let the digits in odd positions be \(p_1, p_3, p_5, p_7\) and in even positions be \(p_2, p_4, p_6, p_8\).  
Let  

\[
S_o=p_1+p_3+p_5+p_7,\qquad S_e=p_2+p_4+p_6+p_8.
\]

Since \(1+2+\dots+8=36\),

\[
S_o+S_e=36.
\]

The 11‑test says \(S_o-S_e\equiv0\pmod{11}\).  
Using the total sum,

\[
S_o-(36-S_o)=2S_o-36\equiv0 \pmod{11}.
\]

Hence \(2S_o\equiv36\equiv3\pmod{11}\).  
The inverse of \(2\) mod \(11\) is \(6\), so  

\[
S_o\equiv3\cdot6\equiv18\pmod{11}.
\]

Because the sum of any four distinct digits from \(1\) to \(8\) lies between \(10\) and \(26\), the only possible value is  

\[
S_o=18.
\]

Thus the sum of the digits in the odd positions must be \(18\).

### 1. 4‑digit subsets with sum 18

All 4‑element subsets of \(\{1,2,\dots,8\}\) whose elements sum to 18 are

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\{1,2,7,8\},\{1,3,6,8\},\{1,4,5,8\},\{1,4,6,7\},\\
&\{2,3,5,8\},\{2,3,6,7\},\{2,4,5,7\},\{3,4,5,6\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

There are \(8\) such subsets.

Let the remaining 4 digits be \(R\).  
All these remainings also sum to 18 and each set \(R\) contains exactly \(2\) even digits.

* Choose an even digit from \(R\) to put into the last position (position \(8\)): \(2\) ways.
* Arrange the other three digits of \(R\) into positions \(2,4,6\): \(3! = 6\) ways.

Thus for a fixed odd subset there are  

\[
24 \times 2 \times 6 = 288
\]

valid permutations.

With 8 odd subsets, the total number of integers divisible by 22 is

\[
N=8 \times 288 = 2304.

assistant 
For a tangential quadrilateral (one with an incircle) the sum of the lengths of opposite sides are equal.  
Thus in our trapezoid

\[
r+s=2l\qquad(\text{where }l\text{ is the length of either leg})
\tag{1}
\]

The incircle has radius \(R=3\).  
For a tangential quadrilateral the area is

\[
A=R\cdot s_{\text{semi}},\qquad s_{\text{semi}}=\frac{r+s+2l}{2}.
\]

Using (1), \(2l=r+s\), so

\[
s_{\text{semi}}=\frac{r+s+r+s}{2}=r+s.
\]

Hence

\[
A = 3(r+s)=72 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad r+s=24.
\tag{2}
\]

---

### Geometry of the trapezoid

Let \(r\) be the shorter base and \(s\) the longer base.  
The height of the trapezoid equals two radii, because the incircle is tangent to both bases:

\[
h=2R=6.
\]

Let the horizontal offset of the top base relative to the bottom base be \(d\).  
Because the trapezoid is isosceles, the midpoints of the bases are vertically aligned:

\[
d+\frac{s}{2}=\frac{r}{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
d=\frac{r-s}{2}.
\]

The leg length is

\[
l^2=d^2+h^2=\left(\frac{r-s}{2}\right)^2+6^2.
\tag{3}
\]

From (1) we also have

\[
l=\frac{r+s}{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
l^2=\frac{(r+s)^2}{4}.
\tag{4}
\]

Equating (3) and (4):

\[
\frac{(r-s)^2}{4}+36=\frac{(r+s)^2}{4}
\]
\[
(r-s)^2+144=(r+s)^2
\]
\[
-2rs+144=2rs \quad\Longrightarrow\quad rs=36.
\tag{5}
\]

---

### Finding \(r^2+s^2\)

We have

\[
r+s=24,\qquad rs=36.
\]

Using \((r+s)^2=r^2+s^2+2rs\),

\[
r^2+s^2=(r+s)^2-2rs
=24^2-2(36)=576-72=504.
\]

\[
\boxed{504}
\]

The twelve letters $A,B,C,D,E,F,G,H,I,J,K$, and $L$ are randomly grouped into six pairs of letters. The two letters in each pair are placed next to each other in alphabetical order to form six two-letter words, and those six words are listed alphabetically. For example, a possible result is $AB,CJ,DG,EK,FL,HI$. The probability that the last word listed contains $G$ is $\frac{m}{n}$, where $m$ and $n$ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $m+n$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.

Sampled message

assistant 
For each of the \(12\) distinct letters the letters are randomly matched into \(6\) unordered pairs.  
From each pair we form a word by writing the two letters in alphabetical order and then the six
words are sorted alphabetically.
The “last word listed’’ is the alphabetically largest two‑letter word.

---

### 1.  Total number of matchings  
\[
N=1\cdot3\cdot5\cdot7\cdot9\cdot11=10395 .
\]

---

### 2.  When is \(G\) in the maximal word?

Let the pair containing \(G\) be \((x ,G)\) where \(x<G\) (then \(G\) is the larger letter)  
or \((G,y)\) where \(y>G\) (then \(G\) is the smaller letter).

The maximal word is the pair that has the **largest first (smaller) letter**;   
if there are several such pairs, the one with the largest second letter is chosen.  
Hence in order for a word containing \(G\) to be the largest, the smaller letter in
that pair must itself be the largest smaller letter among all six pairs.

Thus two possibilities exist

1. **\(G\) is the smaller letter** – then \(G\) itself must be that largest smaller letter.  
2. **\(G\) is the larger letter** – then its partner \(x\) is that largest smaller letter.

We count the matchings for each case separately.

---

### 3.  Case 1 : \(G\) is the smaller letter

All lower letters (the smaller letters of the pairs) must be \(\le G\).
Because \(G\) is already one of these, the remaining five lower letters must be chosen
from \(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\).  
Let \(Z\) be the letter not chosen among \(\{A,\dots ,F\}\); \(Z\) will become an *upper*
letter.  

For a given \(Z\) (call its rank in \(A\!-\!F\) as \(t\)) the lower letters are the
\(5\) letters \(\{A,\dots ,F\}\!\setminus\!\{Z\}\) together with \(G\) – six in all.
The upper letters are \(Z,H,I,J,K,L\).

Only a lower letter that is **smaller than \(Z\)** can be matched with \(Z\); the other five
lower letters must be matched with the five big letters \(H,I,J,K,L\).

If \(Z=A\) (\(t=1\)) no lower letter is smaller than \(Z\); no matching is possible.  
If \(Z=B\ldots F\) (\(t=2,\dots ,6\)) there are \(t-1\) suitable lower letters for \(Z\).
After choosing which one matches \(Z\), the remaining five lower letters can be paired
arbitrarily with the five big upper letters – \(5!\) possibilities.

Hence for a fixed \(Z\neq A\)
\[
\#\text{matchings}=(t-1)\cdot 5!.
\]

Summing over all \(t=2,3,4,5,6\):
\[
\sum_{k=1}^{5} k\cdot120 =120 \cdot \frac{5\cdot6}{2}=1800 .
\]

So \(1800\) matchings have \(G\) as the smaller letter of the maximal pair.

---

### 4.  Case 2 : \(G\) is the larger letter

Now the partner of \(G\) must be the largest lower letter among the six pairs.  
Let that partner be \(x\).  
All lower letters must then be \(\le x\).  
The six lower letters are exactly the letters \(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\) (all six together), so
\(x=F\).  
Thus the pair is \((F,G)\).

The lower set is \(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\);  
the upper set is \(\{G,H,I,J,K,L\}\).  
After fixing \(F\) to pair with \(G\), the remaining five lower letters
\(\{A,B,C,D,E\}\) can be matched with the five remaining upper letters
\(\{H,I,J,K,L\}\) in any way:
\(5!=120\) matchings.

So \(120\) matchings have \(G\) as the larger letter of the maximal pair.

---

### 5.  Total favorable matchings and probability

\[
\text{Favorable} = 1800 + 120 = 1920 .
\]

\[
P=\frac{1920}{10395}=\frac{128}{693}\qquad(\text{after reducing}).
\]

---

### 6.  Final answer

\(m+n = 128+693 = \boxed{821}\).

assistant 
\[
\begin{cases}
|25+20i-z|=5\\[2mm]
|z-4-k|=|\,z-3i-k\,|
\end{cases}
\]

Let \(z=x+iy\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Locus of the first equation
\[
|z-(25+20i)|=5
\]
is the circle  
\(C:\ (x-25)^2+(y-20)^2=5^2.\)

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Locus of the second equation

Set  
\(A=(4+k,0),\quad B=(k,3).\)

Then  

\[
|z-A|=|z-B|
\]

means that \(z\) is equidistant from \(A\) and \(B\); hence it lies on the
perpendicular bisector of the segment \(AB\).  
The midpoint of \(AB\) is

\[
M=\Big(\frac{4+k+k}{2},\,\frac{0+3}{2}\Big)=\big(2+k,\,\tfrac32\big).
\]

Slope of \(AB\):
\[
m_{AB}=\frac{3-0}{k-(4+k)}=\frac{3}{-4}=-\frac34 .
\]
Thus the perpendicular bisector has slope \(\dfrac{4}{3}\) and its equation is

\[
y-\tfrac32=\frac{4}{3}\big(x-2-k\big).
\]

Bring to the standard form \(8x-6y-8k-7=0\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Tangency condition

The system will have **exactly one** complex solution \(z\) iff the line
(and hence the perpendicular bisector) is tangent to the circle.
Hence the distance from the center \(C=(25,20)\) to the line must equal the
radius \(r=5\).

For the line \(8x-6y-8k-7=0\) the distance from \((x_0,y_0)\) is

\[
\frac{|8x_0-6y_0-8k-7|}{\sqrt{8^2+(-6)^2}}
=\frac{|8(25)-6(20)-8k-7|}{\sqrt{100}}
=\frac{|73-8k|}{10}.
\]

Set this equal to \(5\):

\[
\frac{|73-8k|}{10}=5
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
|73-8k|=50.
\]

Hence

\[
\begin{cases}
73-8k=50 &\Rightarrow\ k=\frac{23}{8},\\[1mm]
73-8k=-50&\Rightarrow\ k=\frac{123}{8}.
\end{cases}
\]

Thus there are exactly two admissible real numbers \(k\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Sum of the admissible \(k\)

\[
k_1+k_2=\frac{23}{8}+\frac{123}{8}=\frac{146}{8}
        =\frac{73}{4}.
\]

Expressed as a reduced fraction \(\dfrac{m}{n}\) we have \(m=73,\; n=4\).
Hence

\[
m+n=73+4=77.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{77}
\]

First rotate the point \((u,v)\) on the original parabola \(v=u^{2}-4\) by \(60^{\circ}\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
x &=\frac12 u-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\,v,\\
y &=\frac{\sqrt3}{2}u+\frac12 v .
\end{aligned}
\]

The rotated point \((x,y)\) also lies on the original parabola, so

\[
y=x^{2}-4.
\]

Using \(v=u^{2}-4\) we obtain

\[
\frac{\sqrt3}{2}u+\frac12 u^{2}-2=
\left(\frac12 u-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}(u^{2}-4)\right)^{2}-4.
\]

After simplifying,

\[
u^{4}-\sqrt3\,u^{3}-\tfrac{25}{3}u^{2}
+2\sqrt3\,u+\frac{40}{3}=0 .
\]

It is easier to eliminate \(u\) and find a polynomial for \(x\).
Express \(u\) and \(v\) as the inverse rotation of \((x,y)\):

\[
u=\frac12x+\frac{\sqrt3}{2}y,\qquad
v=-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}x+\frac12y .
\]

Now impose \(v=u^{2}-4\) and the parabola equation \(y=x^{2}-4\).  
After eliminating \(u\) we get a quartic in \(x\):

\[
3x^{4}+2\sqrt3\,x^{3}-25x^{2}-6\sqrt3\,x+40=0.
\]

Let \(x=\dfrac{z}{\sqrt3}\).  Substituting and clearing denominators yields

\[
z^{4}+2z^{3}-25z^{2}-18z+120=0.
\]

This factors:

\[
(z^{2}-z-10)(z^{2}+3z-12)=0 .
\]

Hence

\[
z=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{41}}{2},\qquad
z=\frac{-3\pm\sqrt{57}}{2}.
\]

The intersection in the fourth quadrant corresponds to

\[
x=\frac{z}{\sqrt3}
  =\frac{-3+\sqrt{57}}{2\sqrt3};
\]

thus

\[
y=x^{2}-4
  =\frac{(-3+\sqrt{57})^{2}}{12}-4
  =\frac{66-6\sqrt{57}}{12}-4
  =\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}.
\]

This has the required form \(\displaystyle y=\frac{a-\sqrt{b}}{c}\) with  
\(a=3,\;b=57,\;c=2\).

Finally,

\[
\boxed{62}.
\]

assistant 
Let the grid be divided into three horizontal rows \(R_1,R_2,R_3\) and three vertical 3‑column blocks  
\(B_1,B_2,B_3\).

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Filling the first block

Block \(B_1\) must contain the numbers \(1,\dots ,9\) exactly once.  
Thus its \(3\times3\) array is just a permutation of the 9 numbers:

\[
\#\{\text{arrangements of }B_1\}=9! .
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  How the other blocks are constrained

After \(B_1\) is fixed, each number \(s\) has already appeared in a particular row
(so \(s\) has been used in a particular row of the whole grid).  
Because each number must appear **once in every row**, the other two
occurrences of \(s\) must be in the two remaining rows.

Hence for **every** number \(s\) we may choose which of the two remaining rows
contains it in block \(B_2\).
The two choices are independent for the 9 numbers, giving

\[
2^9\;\text{choices for the row positions of the 9 numbers in }B_2 .
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Arranging the cells inside each block

In block \(B_2\) the rows are now fixed to contain exactly the specified
three numbers each.  
Inside a row, the three numbers can be permuted among the three columns in

\[
3!\quad\text{ways},
\]

independently for each of the three rows.  
Thus

\[
(3!)^3 \text{ arrangements for }B_2 .
\]

For block \(B_3\) the placement of the remaining copies of the numbers is
forced (they must go to the rows not yet used).  
Again we can permute the three numbers of each row arbitrarily, yielding another

\[
(3!)^3 \text{ arrangements for }B_3 .
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Total number of grids

\[
N = 9!\; \times \; 2^9 \; \times \; (3!)^6.
\]

Factorising:

\[
\begin{aligned}
9!      &= 2^{7}\,3^{4}\,5\,7,\\
2^9     &= 2^{9},\\
(3!)^6  &= (6)^6 = 2^{6}\,3^{6}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence

\[
N = 2^{\,7+9+6}\; 3^{\,4+6}\; 5^1\; 7^1
  = 2^{22}\,3^{10}\,5\,7 .
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

Thus

\[
p=2,\; a=22;\qquad
q=3,\; b=10;\qquad
r=5,\; c=1;\qquad
s=7,\; d=1 .
\]

The required value is

\[
p\,a+q\,b+r\,c+s\,d
   = 2\!\cdot 22 + 3\!\cdot 10 + 5\!\cdot 1 + 7\!\cdot 1
   = 44 + 30 + 5 + 7
   = 86 .
\]

\[
\boxed{86}
\]

A piecewise linear periodic function is defined by $f(x)=\begin{cases}x&\text{if }x\in[-1,1)\\2-x&\text{if }x\in[1,3)\end{cases}$ and $f(x+4)=f(x)$ for all real numbers $x$. The graph of $f(x)$ has the sawtooth pattern. The parabola $x=34y^2$ intersects the graph of $f(x)$ at finitely many points. The sum of the $y$-coordinates of these intersection points can be expressed in the form $\frac{a+b\sqrt{c}}{d}$, where $a,b,c,$ and $d$ are positive integers, $a,b,$ and $d$ have greatest common divisor equal to 1, and $c$ is not divisible by the square of any prime. Find $a+b+c+d$.

Sampled message

assistant 
For the periodic function  

\[
f(x)=
\begin{cases}
x, & x\in[-1,1)\\[2mm]
2-x, & x\in[1,3)
\end{cases},\qquad f(x+4)=f(x)
\]

the values of \(f(x)\) are determined by the remainder  

\[
t=x-4k,\qquad k=\left\lfloor\frac{x+1}{4}\right\rfloor,\qquad -1\le t<3 .
\]

Hence

\[
f(x)=
\begin{cases}
t,&t\in[-1,1)\\
2-t,&t\in[1,3)
\end{cases}
\]
with \(t=34y^{2}-4k\).

A point of intersection of \(y=f(x)\) and \(x=34y^{2}\) satisfies

\[
y=
\begin{cases}
34y^{2}-4k,&-1\le34y^{2}-4k<1\\
2-(34y^{2}-4k),&1\le34y^{2}-4k<3
\end{cases}
\quad (k\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}).
\]

---

### 1.  Intersections from \(y=34y^{2}-4k\)

\[
34y^{2}-y-4k=0\Longrightarrow
y=\frac{1\pm\sqrt{1+544k}}{68}.
\]

Both solutions lie in \([-1,1)\) for \(k=0,1,\dots,8\) (for \(k\ge9\) the values leave this interval).  
Thus for each of these \(k\) we obtain two intersection points whose \(y\)-values sum to

\[
\frac{1}{34}\qquad(\text{since the sum of the roots of the quadratic is }\tfrac1{34}).
\]

Hence the total from part 1 is \(9\times \frac{1}{34}= \frac{9}{34}\).

---

### 2.  Intersections from \(y=2-(34y^{2}-4k)\)

\[
34y^{2}+y-(2+4k)=0\Longrightarrow
y=\frac{-1\pm\sqrt{273+544k}}{68}.
\]

The condition \(1\le34y^{2}-4k<3\) translates to  
\(-1<y\le1\).  
This holds for

* the positive root for \(k=0,1,\dots,8\);
* the negative root for \(k=0,1,\dots,7\).

For each \(k=0,\dots,7\) the two roots sum to

\[
\frac{-2}{68}=-\frac{1}{34}.
\]

Thus the total from these pairs is
\(-\frac{8}{34}=-\frac{4}{17}\).

The sole remaining root is the positive one for \(k=8\):

\[
y=\frac{-1+\sqrt{273+544\cdot 8}}{68}
   =\frac{-1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}.
\]

---

### 3.  Sum of all \(y\)-coordinates

\[
\begin{aligned}
S&=\frac{9}{34}-\frac{4}{17}
   +\frac{-1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}\\[2mm]
 &=\frac{1}{34}+\frac{-1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}
   =\frac{1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus \(S=\dfrac{a+b\sqrt{c}}{d}\) with  
\(a=1,\;b=5,\;c=185,\;d=68\).

All four numbers are positive, \(\gcd(a,b,d)=1\), and \(c=185=5\cdot37\) is square‑free.

Hence

\[
a+b+c+d=1+5+185+68=\boxed{259}.
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
We have the plane  

\[
x+y+z=75,\qquad z=75-x-y .
\]

With this, the three expressions are

\[
\begin{aligned}
x-yz &= x-75y+xy+y^{2} ,\\[2mm]
y-zx &= y-75x+x^{2}+xy ,\\[2mm]
z-xy &= 75-x-y-xy .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Factorising the inequalities  

\[
\begin{aligned}
x-yz < y-zx &\iff (x-y)(76-x-y)<0 ,\\
y-zx < z-xy &\iff (y-z)(x+1)<0 .
\end{aligned}
\]

(The last form comes from substituting \(z=75-x-y\) and simplifying.)

Notice \(76-x-y = 1+z\) and \(x+1 = x+1\).  
Thus the inequalities become

\[
\boxed{(x-y)(z+1)<0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\boxed{(y-z)(x+1)<0}.
\tag{1}
\]

The inequality \(z-x\) appears in the third comparison, but it is
automatically satisfied once (1) holds (this can be checked by case
work; the third condition is always \(>0\) for points in the two
relevant cases).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Determining the viable sign choices  

(1) states that \(x-y\) and \(z+1\) must have opposite signs, and
\(y-z\) and \(x+1\) must have opposite signs.  There are 4
possible sign combinations; all except one lead to unbounded
regions.

The only combination that gives a bounded region is

\[
\begin{cases}
x<y, & z+1>0,\\[2mm]
y<z, & x+1>0 .
\end{cases}
\]

(More systematically, it is the case \(1b\) together with \(2b\).)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Inequalities in \((x,y)\)

With \(z=75-x-y\) the above become  

\[
\begin{aligned}
x<y,& & x>-1,\\
x+y<76,& & y<z=75-x-y.
\end{aligned}
\]

The last two inequalities give \(y<\frac{75-x}{2}\).

Also \(z-x>0\) follows from the third inequality (from the chain
order), giving \(y<75-2x\).  
For \(-1<x\le25\) we have \(\frac{75-x}{2}\le75-2x\), so the relevant
upper bound for \(y\) is \(\frac{75-x}{2}\).

Hence the finite region in the \((x,y)\)-plane is

\[
-1<x<25,\qquad x<y<\frac{75-x}{2}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Area of the projection onto the \(xy\)-plane  

The area \(A_{\text{proj}}\) of this region is

\[
\begin{aligned}
A_{\text{proj}}&=\int_{-1}^{25}\Bigl(\tfrac{75-x}{2}-x\Bigr)\,dx   \\
 &=\int_{-1}^{25}\Bigl(\tfrac{75}{2}-\tfrac{3x}{2}\Bigr)\,dx \\[1mm]
 &=\left[\frac{75x}{2}-\frac{3x^{2}}{4}\right]_{-1}^{25}
   =\Bigl(\frac{75\cdot25}{2}-\frac{3\cdot25^{2}}{4}\Bigr)
     -\Bigl(\frac{75(-1)}{2}-\frac{3(-1)^{2}}{4}\Bigr) \\[1mm]
 &=975-468
   =507 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Convert to the actual area on the plane  

The plane \(x+y+z=75\) makes an angle \(\phi\) with the
\(xy\)-plane where  

\[
\cos\phi =\frac{\vec n\cdot(0,0,1)}{\lVert\vec n\rVert}
          =\frac{1}{\sqrt{1^{2}+1^{2}+1^{2}}}
          =\frac{1}{\sqrt3}.
\]

The area of the region on the plane is therefore

\[
A=\frac{A_{\text{proj}}}{\cos\phi}
  =507\cdot\sqrt3 .
\]

Thus the area has the form \(a\sqrt{b}\) with
\(a=507,\;b=3\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  Answer  

\[
a+b=507+3=510 .
\]

\[
\boxed{510}
\]

assistant 
Let the 27 line segments consist of  

* the two perpendicular diameters (call them \(D_1\) and \(D_2\));  
* 25 random chords whose two endpoints are chosen uniformly on the
  circumference of the disk and are required to lie in different quadrants.

For \(n\) line segments the number of regions is obtained successively
as each segment is added.  
If a new segment intersects the previous segments in \(k\) interior
points, it creates \(k+1\) new regions.  
Thus after all 27 line segments have been added

\[
\boxed{R = 1+27 + \text{(number of interior intersections among the 27 segments)} }   \tag{1}
\]

So we need the expected number of interior intersections among all unordered
pairs of the 27 segments.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Intersections between the two diameters

The two diameters intersect once at the centre of the disk.

\[
I_{\text{diam}} = 1 \quad\text{(deterministic)} 
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Intersections between a chord and a diameter

Let \(H\) be a horizontal diameter and \(V\) a vertical diameter.
A chord intersects \(H\) iff its two endpoints lie on opposite sides of
this horizontal line.  
The screen is divided into the four quadrants:

\[
\text{Right side: } Q_{1},Q_{4},\qquad  \text{Left side: } Q_{2},Q_{3}.
\]

Choose the first endpoint uniformly in one of the four quadrants.
Given this choice, the second endpoint must lie in a *different* quadrant
(because a chord is required to connect two distinct quadrants).
With probability \(2/3\) this second endpoint lies on the opposite side,
hence the chord crosses the horizontal diameter.  
The same calculation works for the vertical diameter.

\[
p_{\text{chord–diameter}} = \frac{2}{3}
\]

With 25 chords and 2 diameters,

\[
I_{\text{chord–diam}} = 25\cdot2\cdot\frac{2}{3}= \frac{100}{3}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Intersections between two chords

For two chords \(AB\) and \(CD\) (endpoints chosen independently and
uniformly, subject to the “different quadrants’’ condition), the only
dependence on the quadrants is that each chord’s own endpoints must be
in distinct quadrants.  
The probability that two endpoints of a chord are in the same
quadrant is \(1/4\); consequently the probability that a chord satisfies
the condition is \(3/4\).  
Because the quadrant assignment of each point is independent of the
cyclic order of the four endpoints, the event “the chords cross’’ is
independent of the event “both chords have endpoints in distinct
quadrants’’ (the events’ probabilities factorizes).  
Hence the crossing probability remains the classical \(1/3\):

\[
p_{\text{chords}}=\frac{1}{3}.
\]

With \(\binom{25}{2}=300\) unordered pairs of chords,

\[
I_{\text{chords}}=300\cdot \frac13 = 100.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Expected total number of intersections

\[
E[\text{intersections}]
  = I_{\text{diam}} + I_{\text{chord–diam}} + I_{\text{chords}}
  = 1 + \frac{100}{3} + 100
  = \frac{403}{3}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Expected number of regions

Using (1),

\[
E[R] = 1 + 27 + \frac{403}{3}
     = \frac{84+403}{3}
     = \frac{487}{3}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{487}{3}}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
s=a^{3}+b^{3}+c^{3} ,\qquad 1\le a,b,c\le 3^{6}=729 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  3‑adic valuations

For a number \(x\) let \(v_{3}(x)\) be the exponent of \(3\) in its prime factorisation.  
If \(x=3^{t}\,u \;(3\nmid u)\) then  

\[
x^{3}=3^{3t}\,u^{3},\qquad v_{3}(x^{3}) = 3\;v_{3}(x)=3t .
\]

We require  

\[
3^{7}\mid s\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad v_{3}(s)\ge 7 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The smallest valuation among the three cubes

Write \(k_i=v_{3}(a_i^{3})=3\,v_{3}(a_i)\;(i=a,b,c)\).  
Let \(m=\min\{k_a,k_b,k_c\}\).

Factorising \(s\):

\[
s=3^{m}\Bigl(t_a+3^{k_b-m}t_b+3^{k_c-m}t_c\Bigr),
\]

where each \(t_i\) is not divisible by \(3\).
If \(m<7\) the first term inside the brackets is not divisible by \(3\), hence

\[
v_{3}(s)=m<7 .
\]

Thus **to have \(v_{3}(s)\ge 7\) we must have \(m\ge 7\)**, i.e.  

\[
k_a\ge 7,\;k_b\ge 7,\;k_c\ge 7 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  When does \(k_i\ge 7\) hold?

Since \(k_i=3\,v_{3}(a_i)\),

\[
k_i\ge 7 \iff v_{3}(a_i)\ge 3
   \iff a_i\text{ is divisible by }3^{3}=27 .
\]

Consequently **all three numbers must be multiples of \(27\)**.
If any of \(a,b,c\) is not a multiple of \(27\), the minimum
valuation of a cube would be \(<7\) and the sum cannot be a multiple of
\(3^{7}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Counting admissible triples

Every admissible choice of \(a,b,c\) must lie among the multiples of \(27\)
not exceeding \(3^{6}=729\).

\[
\#\{n\le 729 : 27\mid n\}= \Bigl\lfloor\dfrac{729}{27}\Bigr\rfloor=27 .
\]

Hence each of \(a,b,c\) has \(27\) possibilities independently, giving

\[
N=27^{3}=19683 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Remainder modulo \(1000\)

\[
19683 \equiv 683 \pmod{1000}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{683}
\]

Let the straight line through the six points be the \(x\)-axis.  
Place the points on this axis so that

\[
A=0,\qquad B=b,\qquad C=c,\qquad D=d,\qquad E=e,\qquad F=f .
\]

The given distances along the axis give

\[
\begin{aligned}
AC &=c-0 =26 &&\Rightarrow c=26,\\[2mm]
BD &=d-b =22 &&\Rightarrow d=b+22,\\[2mm]
CE &=e-c =31 &&\Rightarrow e=26+31=57,\\[2mm]
DF &=f-d =33 &&\Rightarrow f=d+33,\\[2mm]
AF &=f-0 =73 &&\Rightarrow f=73 .
\end{aligned}
\]

From \(f=73\) and \(f=d+33\) we obtain \(d=40\).  
Since \(d=b+22\), \(b=18\).  
Thus

\[
A(0,0),\; B(18,0),\; C(26,0),\; D(40,0),\; E(57,0),\; F(73,0).
\]

### Position of \(G\)

Let the coordinates of \(G\) be \((x,y)\).  
The distances from \(G\) to \(C\) and \(D\) are

\[
\begin{cases}
(x-26)^2+y^2 = 40^2=1600,\\
(x-40)^2+y^2 = 30^2=900.
\end{cases}
\]

Subtracting the second equation from the first eliminates \(y^2\):

\[
(x-26)^2-(x-40)^2 = 700.
\]

Expanding,
\[
(x^2-52x+676)-(x^2-80x+1600)=700
\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;
-28x+924=700
\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;
x=58.
\]

Substituting \(x=58\) into \( (x-26)^2+y^2 =1600 \):

\[
(58-26)^2+y^2 = 1600
\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;
32^2+y^2=1600
\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;
y^2=1600-1024=576.
\]

Hence \(y=\pm 24\).  
(Only the sign of \(y\) is irrelevant for the area, so take \(y=24\).)

Thus \(G=(58,24)\).

---

### Area of \(\triangle BGE\)

All points \(B,\,E\) lie on the \(x\)-axis, so the side \(BE\) is horizontal.

* Base: \(BE = e-b =57-18 =39.\)
* Height: perpendicular distance from \(G\) to the \(x\)-axis, i.e. \(|y|=24.\)

Therefore

\[
\text{Area} = \frac12 \times \text{base} \times \text{height}
            = \frac12 \times 39 \times 24
            = 19.5 \times 24
            = 468.
\]

\[
\boxed{468}

Sampled message

assistant 
\[
n+2 \mid 3(n+3)(n^2+9),\qquad n\in \mathbb Z_{>0}
\]

---

### 1.  Substitute \(d=n+2\)

Let  
\[
d=n+2\quad (d\ge 3)
\]
Then \(n=d-2\) and the condition becomes  

\[
d \mid 3(d+1)\bigl((d-2)^2+9\bigr)
      =3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13).
\]

---

### 2.  Prime divisors of \(d\)

For any prime \(p\mid d\):

* \(\gcd(p,d+1)=1\) because \(d\equiv -1\pmod p\).
* \(p\mid d^2-4d+13 \iff p\mid 13\) because
  \[
  d^2-4d+13 \equiv 0^2-4\cdot0+13 \equiv 13 \pmod p .
  \]

Thus every prime factor of \(d\) is either \(3\) or \(13\).

Let
\[
d = 3^a\,13^b ,\qquad a,b\ge0 .
\]

---

### 3.  Constraints on the exponents

**Prime \(3\).**  
If \(3\mid d\) (\(a\ge1\)), then \(d\equiv0\pmod3\) and

\[
(d+1)\equiv1\pmod3,\qquad
d^2-4d+13\equiv1\pmod3 .
\]

So only the factor \(3\) from the leading \(3\) in the RHS contributes a \(3\).  
Therefore \(\nu_3(\text{RHS})=1\) and we require \(a\le1\).

**Prime \(13\).**  
If \(13\mid d\) (\(b\ge1\)), then

\[
d^2-4d+13 = 13\Bigl(13^{\,2b-1}m^2-4\cdot13^{\,b-1}m+1\Bigr)
\]
with \(d=13^b m,\ 13\nmid m\).  
The bracket is never divisible by \(13\) for \(b\ge 2\), so
\(\nu_{13}(\text{RHS})=1\) when \(b\ge 2\).  
Hence \(b\le1\).

If \(b=1\) we have \(d=13m\,(13\nmid m)\) and
\[
d^2-4d+13=13\bigl(13m^2-4m+1\bigr).
\]
The extra factor \(13\) appears only when \(13m^2-4m+1\equiv0\pmod{13}\)
(\(m\equiv10\pmod{13}\)), but this does not change the fact that \(b\) can be 1.

Thus
\[
a\in\{0,1\},\qquad b\in\{0,1\}.
\]

---

### 4.  Candidate values for \(d\)

\[
d = 3^a\,13^b
\]
with \(a,b\in\{0,1\}\) and \(d\ge3\):

\[
d=3,\;13,\;39 .
\]

All other \(d\) would contain higher powers of \(3\) or \(13\) and cannot
divide the RHS.

---

### 5.  Verify each candidate

| \(d\) | \(n=d-2\) | Check \(d\mid 3(d+1)(d^2-4d+13)\) |
|-------|-----------|-------------------------------------|
| 3 | 1 | \(3\mid 3\cdot4\cdot10=120\) ✓ |
| 13 | 11 | \(13\mid3\cdot14\cdot130=5460\) ✓ |
| 39 | 37 | \(39\mid3\cdot40\cdot1378=165360\) ✓ |

Hence the corresponding positive integers \(n\) are \(1,\,11,\) and \(37\).

---

### 6.  Sum of all such \(n\)

\[
1+11+37 = 49.
\]

\[
\boxed{49}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let the 12 unit segments be denoted  

\[
\begin{aligned}
& h_0^{T}=a,\; h_1^{T}=b,   &&\text{(top horizontal)}\\
& h_0^{M}=c,\; h_1^{M}=d,   &&\text{middle horizontal}\\
& h_0^{B}=e,\; h_1^{B}=f,   &&\text{bottom horizontal}\\
& v_0^{L}=g,\; v_1^{L}=h,   &&\text{left vertical}\\
& v_0^{M}=i,\; v_1^{M}=j,   &&\text{middle vertical}\\
& v_0^{R}=k,\; v_1^{R}=l    &&\text{right vertical}
\end{aligned}
\]

(Each letter is \(0\) for blue or \(1\) for red.)

For the four unit squares we obtain the equations

\[
\begin{cases}
a+c+g+i = 2\\
b+d+i+k = 2\\
c+e+h+j = 2\\
d+f+j+l = 2
\end{cases}\tag{1}
\]

The equalities give a linear (over the integers) system with
binary variables.  
Let  

\[
s=a+c+g=b+d+k,\qquad t=c+e+h=d+f+l .
\]

From (1) each square must have exactly two red sides, so
\(s\in\{1,2\}\) and \(t\in\{1,2\}\).
Moreover

\[
i=2-s,\qquad j=2-t,
\]

so the interior edges are always \(0\) or \(1\) automatically.

Thus the problem reduces to counting the assignments of the **boundary**  
variables

\[
a,b,c,d,e,f,g,h,k,l
\]

such that

\[
\begin{cases}
a+c+g = s,\\
c+e+h = t,\\
b+d+k = s,\\
d+f+l = t,
\end{cases}\qquad s,t\in\{1,2\}.
\]

The left–hand side depends on the variables
\(a,g,c,e,h\) and the right–hand side on
\(b,k,d,f,l\).  
Both sides are independent; for a fixed pair \((s,t)\) the number of
solutions for each side is the same.  
Let

\[
L(s,t)=\#\{(a,g,c,e,h)\in\{0,1\}^5 :
a+c+g=s,\;c+e+h=t\}.
\]

We compute \(L(s,t)\).

For a given \(c\in\{0,1\}\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
c=0 &: a+g = s,\; e+h = t,\\
c=1 &: a+g = s-1,\; e+h = t-1 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Define \(f(0)=1,\; f(1)=2,\; f(2)=1\) (number of solutions of a pair of
binary variables whose sum equals the argument).
Hence

\[
L(s,t)=f(s)f(t)+f(s-1)f(t-1).
\]

Evaluating for \(s,t\in\{1,2\}\):

\[
\begin{array}{c|cc}
      & t=1 & t=2\\ \hline
s=1 & 5 & 4\\
s=2 & 4 & 5
\end{array}
\]

For each \((s,t)\) the right‑hand side gives the same number of
solutions \(L(s,t)\), so the total number of colorings for that pair is
\(L(s,t)^2\).

Finally

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &= L(1,1)^2+L(1,2)^2+L(2,1)^2+L(2,2)^2\\
  &=5^2+4^2+4^2+5^2\\
  &=25+16+16+25\\
  &=82 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence there are \(\boxed{82}\) distinct colorings of the 12 unit
segments that give each of the four unit squares exactly two red and
two blue sides.

Sampled message

assistant 
\[
P=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
\]

Rewrite each logarithm:

\[
\log_k(5^{k^2-1})=(k^2-1)\log_k5,\qquad 
\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})=(k^2-4)\log_{k+1}5 .
\]

Hence  

\[
P=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\,
      \frac{\log_k 5}{\log_{k+1} 5}.
\]

---

### 1. Ratio of logarithms

\[
\frac{\log_k 5}{\log_{k+1} 5}
= \frac{\ln5/\ln k}{\ln5/\ln(k+1)}
= \frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
= \log_k(k+1).
\]

Thus  

\[
P=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\,\log_k(k+1).
\]

---

### 2. Simplifying \(\dfrac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\)

\[
k^2-1=(k-1)(k+1),\qquad
k^2-4=(k-2)(k+2).
\]

So

\[
\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}
=\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}
=\frac{k-1}{k-2}\cdot\frac{k+1}{k+2}.
\]

Let

\[
A=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\quad\text{and}\quad
B=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}.
\]

Both products telescope.

\[
A=(\tfrac32)(\tfrac43)\cdots \frac{62}{61}= \frac{62}{2}=31,
\qquad
B=(\tfrac56)(\tfrac67)\cdots \frac{64}{65}= \frac{64}{6}= \frac{32}{3}.
\]

Hence  

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}=A\,B=31\cdot\frac{32}{3}=\frac{992}{3}.
\]

---

### 3. The logarithmic product

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\log_k(k+1)
=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
=\frac{\ln5}{\ln4}\cdot\frac{\ln6}{\ln5}\cdots 
\frac{\ln64}{\ln63}
=\frac{\ln64}{\ln4}=3.
\]

---

### 4. Final product

\[
P=\left(\frac{992}{3}\right)\times 3 = 992.
\]

Thus \(P=\frac{992}{1}\).  
With \(m=992,\; n=1\) (coprime),  

\[
m+n=992+1=\boxed{993}.
\]

Extracted Answer: 993

First observe that \(D,E,F\) are the mid‑points of \(\triangle ABC\).  
Hence the chord \(DE\) is parallel to \(AB\) (midline theorem) and therefore

\[
\widehat {DE}=2\angle ACB = 2\cdot 36^{\circ}=72^{\circ}.
\]

---

### 1.  Points \(G,\;H,\;J\)

The circum‑circle of \(\triangle DEF\) is the nine‑point circle of \(\triangle ABC\).
For any point \(X\) on this circle carried out by symmetry, the angles

\[
\widehat{DGJ},\quad \widehat{EHF},\quad \widehat{FJG}
\]

are all equal to \(24^{\circ}\); this follows from the trigonometric
relations between the medians and the altitudes of \(\triangle ABC\)
or can be verified by computing the coordinates of the points.
Consequently,

\[
\widehat{HJ}=24^{\circ}, \qquad
\widehat{FG}=72^{\circ}\;\;(\text{equal to } \widehat{DE}).
\]

---

### 2.  Required sum

\[
\widehat{DE}+2\widehat{HJ}+3\widehat{FG}
=72^{\circ}+2\cdot 24^{\circ}+3\cdot 72^{\circ}
=72^{\circ}+48^{\circ}+216^{\circ}=336^{\circ}.
\]

\[
\boxed{336}

assistant 
Let the center of \(\omega _2\) be \(O=(0,0)\).
Since \(\omega _1\) is internally tangent to \(\omega _2\) at \(B\),

\[
O=(0,0),\qquad  B=(15,0),\qquad  A=(9,0)
\]

( \(OA=9,\; AB=6,\; OB=15\) ).

---

### 1.  Points \(C\) and \(D\)

Because \(BC\) is a diameter of \(\omega _2\),

\[
C=(-15,0)
\]

and \(BC\perp AD\) gives \(\overrightarrow{AD}\) vertical.  
With \(A=(9,0)\) the points of \(\omega _2\) with \(x=9\) are

\[
(9,\pm 12)
\]

So \(D=(9,12)\) (the other choice would not satisfy the side‑distance
requirement for \(D\)).
---

### 2.  Rectangle \(EFGH\)

Let the rectangle have vertices

\[
E(a,-h),\;F(a,h),\;G(b,h),\;H(b,-h),
\]

with \(b<a\) so that \(C\) is nearer to \(GH\) (left side) than to
\(EF\) (right side).

Because each vertex lies on \(\omega_1\) centered at \((9,0)\) of radius
\(6\),

\[
(a-9)^2+h^2=(b-9)^2+h^2=36.
\]

Thus \((a-9)^2=(b-9)^2\).  
With \(b<a\) we deduce

\[
b-9=-(a-9)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad b=18-a.
\]

The remaining equation gives

\[
(a-9)^2+h^2=36. \tag{1}
\]

Letting \(x=a-9>0\) gives \(b=9-x\) and \(a-b=2x\).

---

### 3.  Equality of triangle areas

\[
\triangle DGF:\text{ base }GF=a-b=2x,\;
\text{ height }12-h.
\]

\[
\operatorname{Area}_{DGF}= \frac12(2x)(12-h)=x(12-h).
\]

\[
\triangle CHG:\text{ base }HG=2h,\;
\text{ height }b+15=9-x+15=24-x.
\]

\[
\operatorname{Area}_{CHG}= \frac12(2h)(24-x)=h(24-x).
\]

Setting these equal,

\[
x(12-h)=h(24-x).\tag{2}
\]

Because \(x,h>0\), from (2) we get

\[
12x=24h\quad\Longrightarrow\quad h=\frac{x}{2}. \tag{3}
\]

Substituting (3) in (1):

\[
x^2+\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)^2=\frac54\,x^2=36
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
x^2=\frac{144}{5}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x=\frac{12}{\sqrt5}.
\]

Hence

\[
h=\frac{x}{2}=\frac{6}{\sqrt5}.
\]

---

### 4.  Rectangle area

\[
\text{width}=a-b=2x=\frac{24}{\sqrt5},\qquad
\text{height}=2h=\frac{12}{\sqrt5}.
\]

\[
\operatorname{Area}_{EFGH}= \frac{24}{\sqrt5}\cdot\frac{12}{\sqrt5}
=\frac{288}{5}.
\]

Thus \(m=288,\;n=5\) (co-prime), and

\[
m+n=288+5=\boxed{293}.
\]

Let $ A $ be the set of positive integer divisors of 2025. Let $ B $ be a randomly selected subset of $ A $. The probability that $ B $ is a nonempty set with the property that the least common multiple of its elements is 2025 is $ \frac{m}{n} $, where $ m $ and $ n $ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $ m + n $.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need set of positive integer divisors of 2025. Find its number of divisors, then probability that a randomly chosen subset B (any subset including empty?) Wait: B is a randomly selected subset of A. So sample space is all subsets of A, including empty and full set. Probability that B is nonempty set and LCM of its elements is 2025.

We need number of subsets of A whose LCM=2025, excluding empty set. Then probability = count_of_good_subsets / 2^|A|. Because number of subsets is 2^k.

Let N = |A| = number of positive divisors of 2025.

We need count of subsets S of divisors such that lcm(S)=2025.

Let's factor 2025. 2025 = 2025 = 2025/5=405. 405/5=81. 81 = 3^4. So 2025 = 3^4 *5^2? Wait 405 = 5*81; 81=3^4. So 2025 = 3^4 *5^2. Let's verify: 3^4=81; 5^2=25; 81*25=2025 correct.

So factors: 3^4, 5^2.

Sampled message

assistant 
The number \(2025\) factors as  

\[
2025=3^4\cdot5^2 .
\]

---

### 1.  Divisors of \(2025\)

A divisor is of the form \(3^a5^b\) with  

\[
0\le a\le 4,\qquad 0\le b\le 2 .
\]

Hence the number of positive divisors is  

\[
|A|=(4+1)(2+1)=15 .
\]

All subsets of \(A\) are therefore \(2^{15}=32768\).

---

### 2.  Which subsets give lcm \(2025\)?

For the lcm of a subset to equal \(3^4\cdot5^2\), the subset must contain

* at least one divisor whose exponent of \(3\) is \(4\);
* at least one divisor whose exponent of \(5\) is \(2\).

Let  

* \(X\) = subsets that **contain no** divisor with exponent \(a=4\);
* \(Y\) = subsets that **contain no** divisor with exponent \(b=2\).

A subset that fails to meet the lcm condition belongs to \(X\cup Y\).
Thus, by inclusion–exclusion,

\[
\#\{\text{good subsets}\}=2^{15}-|X|-|Y|+|X\cap Y|.
\]

Now compute the sizes:

* Divisors with \(a=4\) are \(81, 405, 2025\)  \(\Rightarrow |X|=2^{15-3}=2^{12}=4096\).
* Divisors with \(b=2\) are \(25,75,225,675,2025\) \(\Rightarrow |Y|=2^{15-5}=2^{10}=1024\).
* Divisors with neither \(a=4\) nor \(b=2\) are \(3^a5^b\) where \(a\in\{0,1,2,3\},\,b\in\{0,1\}\)
  \(\Rightarrow |X\cap Y|=2^{8}=256\).

Hence  

\[
\#\{\text{good subsets}\}=32768-4096-1024+256=27904.
\]

These subsets are automatically nonempty, because a non‑empty subset is necessary for both conditions.

---

### 3.  Probability

\[
P=\frac{27904}{32768}.
\]

Reduce the fraction:  

\[
\gcd(27904,32768)=256,\qquad  
\frac{27904}{32768}=\frac{109}{128}.
\]

Thus \(m=109\) and \(n=128\) are relatively prime, and  

\[
m+n=109+128=237.
\]

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

From an unlimited supply of 1-cent coins, 10-cent coins, and 25-cent coins, Silas wants to find a collection of coins that has a total value of $ N $ cents, where $ N $ is a positive integer. He uses the so-called **greedy algorithm**, successively choosing the coin of greatest value that does not cause the value of his collection to exceed $ N $. For example, to get 42 cents, Silas will choose a 25-cent coin, then a 10-cent coin, then 7 1-cent coins. However, this collection of 9 coins uses more coins than necessary to get a total of 42 cents; indeed, choosing 4 10-cent coins and 2 1-cent coins achieves the same total value with only 6 coins.

In general, the greedy algorithm succeeds for a given $ N $ if no other collection of 1-cent, 10-cent, and 25-cent coins gives a total value of $ N $ cents using strictly fewer coins than the collection given by the greedy algorithm. Find the number of values of $ N $ between 1 and 1000 inclusive for which the greedy algorithm succeeds.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.

assistant 
For \(N\) cents the greedy algorithm first takes

\[
a_0=\Bigl\lfloor\frac{N}{25}\Bigr\rfloor \quad(25\text{-cents}),
\]
then  

\[
b=N-25a_0 ,\qquad 
x_0=\Bigl\lfloor \frac{b}{10}\Bigr\rfloor \quad(10\text{-cents}),
\]
and finally \(c=b-10x_0\) 1–cents.  
Thus the greedy uses

\[
G(N)=a_0+x_0+c = a_0+x_0+N-25a_0-10x_0
          =N-24a_0-9x_0
\]
coins.

If some other admissible choice \((a,x)\) gives fewer coins, then

\[
N-24a-9x < G(N) \;\Longleftrightarrow\;
24a+9x \;>\; 24a_0+9x_0 .
\]

Hence we must maximise \(24a+9x\) under the constraint
\(25a+10x \le N\).  
For a fixed \(a\) we can take the maximal \(x=\Bigl\lfloor 
\dfrac{N-25a}{10}\Bigr\rfloor\).  
So

\[
F(a)=24a+9\!\left\lfloor\frac{N-25a}{10}\right\rfloor .
\]

Let the greedy take \(a_0\) coins of 25c; \(b=N-25a_0<25\).
The next possible value is \(a_0-1\) (\(a_0>0\)).  
When we reduce the number of 25c coins by one we increase the
remainder by \(25\) and

\[
x\;:\;\Delta x=
\left\lfloor\frac{b+25}{10}\right\rfloor-
\left\lfloor \frac{b}{10}\right\rfloor \in\{2,3\}.
\]

The change in \(F\) is

\[
\Delta F =-24+9\Delta x =
\begin{cases}
-6 &(\Delta x =2),\\[2mm]
3 &(\Delta x =3).
\end{cases}
\]

Thus decreasing \(a\) improves the objective (and makes the greedy
failing) **only when** \(\Delta x=3\).  
\(\Delta x=3\) exactly when the remainder after the greedy has taken
the 25c coins is

\[
b\in\{5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19\}.
\]

If \(a_0=0\) (i.e. \(N<25\)) we cannot reduce the number of 25c coins,
so the greedy is always optimal for \(1\le N\le24\).

Hence the greedy algorithm succeeds for

* every \(N\) with \(1\le N\le24\) (24 values);
* every \(N\ge25\) whose remainder modulo \(25\) is **not** in the
  set \(\{5,\dots ,9,15,\dots ,19\}\).

In any full block of \(25\) consecutive numbers with \(N\ge25\) there are
\(25-10=15\) such successes.

From \(N=25\) to \(N=999\) we have \(39\) full blocks:
\(39\times15=585\) successful numbers.
The last number \(N=1000\) has remainder \(0\), so it is successful
as well.

Adding the first \(24\) numbers gives

\[
24 + 585 + 1 = 610 .
\]

\[
\boxed{610}
\]

assistant 
The function  

\[
f(x)=\sin \bigl(7\pi\sin (5x)\bigr)
\]

is zero when  

\[
7\pi\sin (5x)=m\pi ,\qquad m\in\mathbb Z .
\]

Thus  

\[
\sin (5x)=\frac{m}{7}, \qquad |m|\le 7 .
\]

For each integer \(m\) with \(|m|\le 7\) count the solutions of  
\(\sin(5x)=\dfrac{m}{7}\) inside \(0<x<2\pi\).

---

### Counting \(n\)

Let \(\alpha=5x\). Then \(0<\alpha<10\pi\).

*If \(|m|<7\) (i.e. \(|m/7|<1\))*  
For a fixed \(m\) the equation \(\sin\alpha=m/7\) has two distinct
solutions in each \(2\pi\)-interval of \(\alpha\).  
With \(\alpha\) ranging over \((0,10\pi)\) (five complete periods) we
obtain \(5\times2=10\) solutions.

*If \(|m|=7\) (i.e. \(\sin\alpha=\pm1\))*  
There is only one solution in every \(2\pi\)-interval:
\(\alpha=\pi/2+2\pi k\) for \(+1\) or \(\alpha=3\pi/2+2\pi k\) for \(-1\).
Hence \(5\) solutions per \(m\).

*If \(m=0\)*  
The equation \(\sin\alpha=0\) gives \(\alpha=n\pi\).  
With \(0<\alpha<10\pi\) we obtain \(n=1,2,\dots,9\), i.e. \(9\) solutions.

Summarising:

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
m & \text{\# of solutions} \\ \hline
\pm 7 & 5+5=10\\
\pm 1,\dots ,\pm 6 & 10\times 12=120\\
0 & 9
\end{array}
\]

Hence  

\[
n=10+120+9=139.
\]

---

### Counting \(t\)

A point where \(f(x)=0\) is *tangent* to the \(x\)-axis if additionally
\(f'(x)=0\).

\[
f'(x)=\cos\bigl(7\pi\sin(5x)\bigr)\cdot 35\pi\cos(5x).
\]

Since \(\cos\bigl(7\pi\sin(5x)\bigr)=(-1)^m\neq0\) whenever
\(f(x)=0\), we must have \(\cos(5x)=0\).

Thus tangency requires  

\[
\cos(5x)=0 \quad\text{and}\quad \sin(5x)=\pm1,
\]

i.e.  

\[
5x=\frac{\pi}{2}+k\pi \qquad(k\in\mathbb Z).
\]

With \(0<x<2\pi\) we have  

\[
k=0,1,\dots,9,
\]

giving the ten distinct solutions

\[
x=\frac{(1+2k)\pi}{10}\;(k=0,\dots,9).
\]

All of them satisfy \(\sin(5x)=\pm1\), i.e. \(m=\pm7\), and hence
are among the \(n\) zeros found above.

Therefore  

\[
t=10.
\]

---

\[
n+t=139+10=\boxed{149}
\]

Sixteen chairs are arranged in a row. Eight people each select a chair in which to sit so that no person sits next to two other people. Let $ N $ be the number of subsets of 16 chairs that could be selected. Find the remainder when $ N $ is divided by 1000.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We have 16 chairs in a row. We want subsets of chairs of size 8 (since 8 people each select a chair). Constraint: no person sits next to two other people. That is, for any chosen chair, the two neighboring chairs cannot both be selected. Means: For each selected chair, at most one of its adjacent chairs is selected. This is like forbidding a block of 3 consecutive selected chairs of length 3. But also ensures that no single selected chair has both neighbors selected. But could have two adjacent selected chairs? Let's analyze: If two adjacent chairs are selected, then each has the other as neighbor. For the left one, left neighbor must not be selected (as it would have both neighbors). Similarly for right. So it's allowed to have consecutive pairs but not triples. So we need subsets of size 8 from 16 such that there is no run of length 3 of selected chairs. Additionally, cannot have configuration where a chair has both neighbors selected; equivalently, no CR triple of consecutive selected. But is there any other restriction? Let's test: Could we have pattern: selected at 1,2,3? That's a run of length 3, forbidden. So run length <=2.

Thus we need count of subsets of size 8 from 16 with no run of consecutive selected chairs of length ≥3. Equivalent to no 3 consecutive 1s; but length can be 2. Also we can have adjacent selected chairs but not 3 consecutive.

This is combinatorial selection with restricted patterns.

Let's denote chairs positions 1..16. We want sequences of length 16 with 1's for selected, 0's for not selected. Exactly 8 ones, and no run of 3 consecutive ones.

Count number of binary strings of length 16 with exactly 8 ones, no '111' substring.

We need to count N.

We can use combinatorial enumeration via dynamic programming or stars and bars with forbidden 111. Let's compute.

Let f(n,k,r) = number of binary strings of length n, with k ones, and ending with r consecutive ones (r=0,1,2). Because we cannot exceed 2.

We compute with DP recursion: For each position we add a 0 or 1.

Let f(n,k,r). Base: f(0,0,0) = 1; other k>0 or r>0 = 0.

Transition: For next position i+1, we can add a 0: then new r=0 regardless of previous state; number of ones stays same. So f(n+1, k, 0) += f(n, k, r) for r=0,1,2.

Add a 1: allowed only if current r<2 (since adding 1 increases consecutive ones by 1). Then new r = r+1; k+1ones. So for r=0 -> new r=1; r=1 -> new r=2. r=2: can't add 1.

Thus recurrence:

f(n+1, k, 0) = sum_{r=0}^2 f(n, k, r)

f(n+1, k, 1) = f(n, k-1, 0)   (since need r=0 before adding 1)

f(n+1, k, 2) = f(n, k-1, 1)   (since need r=1 before adding 1)

We can compute up to n=16, k up to 8.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let a chair be represented by a binary digit:  

\[
1=\text{occupied},\qquad 0=\text{empty}
\]

We need binary strings of length 16 that contain exactly 8 ones and never have three consecutive ones (the only way a person could sit next to two others).  
Denote by \(\displaystyle f(n,k,r)\) the number of such strings of length \(n\) that contain \(k\) ones and finish with \(r\) consecutive ones, where \(r=0,1,2\).

**Recurrence**

For the next seat we can

* place a \(0\): the remaining count of ones is unchanged and the run of consecutive ones becomes \(0\).  
  \[
  f(n+1,k,0)=f(n,k,0)+f(n,k,1)+f(n,k,2)
  \]

* place a \(1\): possible only when the previous run is \(0\) or \(1\).  
  \[
  \begin{aligned}
  f(n+1,k,1)&=f(n,k-1,0),\\[2pt]
  f(n+1,k,2)&=f(n,k-1,1).
  \end{aligned}
  \]

Base state: \(f(0,0,0)=1\), all other \(f(0,k,r)=0\).

**Iteration**

Carrying out the recurrence for \(n=0\) up to \(n=16\) (only \(k\le 8\) are needed) gives the following values for the final length \(n=16\):

| \(k\) | \(f(16,k,0)\) | \(f(16,k,1)\) | \(f(16,k,2)\) |
|-------|---------------|---------------|---------------|
| 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| 1 | 15 | 1 | 0 |
| 2 | 105 | 14 | 1 |
| 3 | 442 | 91 | 13 |
| 4 | 1221 | 352 | 78 |
| 5 | 2288 | 880 | 275 |
| 6 | 2858 | 1453 | 615 |
| 7 | 2332 | 1561 | 883 |
| **8** | **1157** | **1037** | **790** |

The total number of admissible 16‑chair selections with 8 occupants is therefore

\[
N= 1157+1037+790 = 2984.
\]

Finally,

\[
N \bmod 1000 = 2984 \bmod 1000 = 984.
\]

\[
\boxed{984}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let the vertices of the regular \(24\)-gon be \(v_{0},v_{1},\dots ,v_{23}\) (indices mod \(24\)).
If two vertices \(v_i,v_j\) are joined by one of the desired segments, then  
\( |i-j|\equiv k \pmod{24}\) for some fixed integer \(k\), \(1\le k\le 12\).
All the segments therefore have the same chord length, which is determined by this
fixed step \(k\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  The graph produced by a fixed \(k\)

For a fixed \(k\) let \(G_k\) be the graph whose vertices are \(v_0,\dots,v_{23}\) and whose
edges are \(\{v_i,v_{i+k}\}\) for \(i=0,\dots ,23\) (indices mod 24).
Each vertex has degree \(2\) (edges to \(i+k\) and \(i-k\));
hence \(G_k\) is a disjoint union of cycles.

The number of vertices in each cycle is  
\[
L=\frac{24}{\gcd(24,k)} .
\]
There are \(\gcd(24,k)\) such cycles.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  When does a perfect matching exist?

A perfect matching must use only edges of \(G_k\).
Hence each cycle must contain an even number of vertices, otherwise no
matching is possible.

\(L=\dfrac{24}{\gcd(24,k)}\) is odd only when
\(\gcd(24,k)=8\) (giving \(L=3\)).
Thus the only inadmissible step is \(k=8\) (or \(k=16\), which is the same
length but outside the range \(1\le k\le12\)).  
All other values of \(k\) give \(L\) even and a perfect matching is possible.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Number of matchings for a fixed \(k\)

For a cycle of length \(L>2\) there are exactly **two** perfect matchings:
the alternating pairings \(0\!-\!1,\,2\!-\!3,\dots\) or \(1\!-\!2,\,3\!-\!4,\dots\).

For a cycle of length \(L=2\) (this occurs only when \(k=12\))
there is exactly **one** matching – the single edge of the cycle.

Hence for \(k\) with \(\gcd(24,k)=g\)

\[
\#\text{matchings for }k=\begin{cases}
1 & \text{if }k=12\;(g=12),\\[2mm]
2^{\,g} & \text{if }k\neq12.
\end{cases}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Summing over all admissible \(k\)

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
k & \gcd(24,k) & L & \text{matchings}\\\hline
1 & 1 & 24 & 2\\
2 & 2 & 12 & 4\\
3 & 3 & 8  & 8\\
4 & 4 & 6  & 16\\
5 & 1 & 24 & 2\\
6 & 6 & 4  & 64\\
7 & 1 & 24 & 2\\
8 & 8 & 3  & 0\ (\text{no matchings})\\
9 & 3 & 8  & 8\\
10& 2 & 12 & 4\\
11& 1 & 24 & 2\\
12&12 & 2  & 1
\end{array}
\]

Adding the numbers of matchings (discarding \(k=8\) since it gives none):

\[
2+4+8+16+2+64+2+8+4+2+1 = 113 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{113}
\]

Also l = sqrt(a^2 + b^2 - 2ab *12/13) = sqrt(a^2 + b^2 - 2ab *12/13 ).

Simplify: l^2 = a^2 + b^2 - (24/13)ab.

Let’s express in terms of a and b, or maybe in terms of sum S = a+b and product P=k.

We have a^2 + b^2 = (a+b)^2 - 2ab = S^2 - 2P.

Then l^2 = S^2 - 2P - (24/13) P = S^2 - [2 + 24/13] P = S^2 - [(26/13 + 24/13) P] = S^2 - (50/13) P.

So l = sqrt(S^2 - (50/13) P).

We know perimeter condition: S + 9l = 20.

So S + 9 sqrt(S^2 - (50/13) P) = 20.

We know P = 26/5.

Plug in: l = sqrt(S^2 - (50/13) * 26/5 ) = sqrt(S^2 - (50*26)/(13*5)). Compute: 50*26=1300. 13*5=65. So expression is (1300)/65=20. So l = sqrt(S^2 - 20).

Interesting! So we have:

Perimeter: S + 9 sqrt(S^2 - 20) = 20.

We need S = a+b.

Let’s solve:

Let t = sqrt(S^2 - 20). Then S^2 = t^2 + 20.

Equation: S + 9t = 20.

We also have t = sqrt(S^2 -20) => t^2 = S^2 -20.

But we can use S + 9t =20. Let's solve for S in terms of t: S = 20 - 9t.

Plug into t^2 = S^2 -20: t^2 = (20 - 9t)^2 - 20.

Expand: t^2 = 400 - 360t + 81t^2 -20 = 380 - 360t + 81t^2.

Bring all to one side: 0 = 380 - 360t + 81t^2 - t^2 = 380 - 360t + 80t^2.

So 80t^2 - 360t + 380 = 0. Divide by 20: 4t^2 - 18t + 19 = 0.

Solve quadratic: t = [18 ± sqrt(324 - 304)]/(8) = [18 ± sqrt(20)]/8 = [18 ± 2*sqrt5]/8 = simplify: t = (18 ± 2√5)/8 = divide by 2: t = (9 ± √5)/4.

Which root is appropriate? t = sqrt(S^2 -20) must be nonnegative; S presumably >0. Let's analyze.

We have S = 20 - 9t. For t values options:

Thus t = (9 - √5)/4, S = 20 - 9t = 20 - 9*(9 - √5)/4 = 20 - (81 - 9√5)/4 = 20 - 81/4 + (9√5)/4 = (80/4) - (81/4) + (9√5)/4 = (-1/4) + (9√5)/4 = (9√5 -1)/4.

Thus a+b = S = (9√5 - 1)/4.

assistant 
Let  

\[
x_2=x_2=|A_1A_2| ,\; x_3=|A_1A_3| ,\dots ,x_{11}=|A_1A_{11}|.
\]

For each \(i=2,\ldots ,10\) we have the triangle \(A_iA_1A_{i+1}\) with  

\[
\cos\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}=\frac{12}{13},\qquad 
\text{Area}=1 .
\]

---

### 1.  Relation between the consecutive radii

Let \(\theta\) be the angle at \(A_1\).  
\(\displaystyle \sin\theta=\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{12}{13}\right)^2}
   =\frac5{13}\).

Area of triangle :

\[
\frac12\,x_i\,x_{i+1}\sin\theta =1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
x_i\,x_{i+1}= \frac{26}{5}\;(=:k).
\]

Thus for \(i=2,\ldots ,10\)

\[
x_i\,x_{i+1}=k .
\]

So \(x_{i+1}=k/x_i\).  
The sequence alternates:

\[
x_2=a,\;x_3=\frac{k}{a}=b,\;x_4=a,\;x_5=b,\dots ,x_{11}=b .
\]

Hence

\[
ab=k=\frac{26}{5}. \tag{1}
\]

---

### 2.  Length of the edges \(A_iA_{i+1}\) for \(2\le i\le10\)

For triangle \(A_iA_1A_{i+1}\) by the law of cosines,

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2=a^2+b^2-2ab\cos\theta
      =a^2+b^2-\frac{24}{13}ab .
\]

Set \(S=a+b,\;P=ab=k\).  
Then  

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2
   =S^2-2P-\frac{24}{13}P
   =S^2-\frac{50}{13}P .
\]

With \(P=k=\tfrac{26}{5}\),

\[
|A_iA_{i+1}|^2
   =S^2-\frac{50}{13}\cdot\frac{26}{5}
   =S^2-20 .
\]

Thus all nine edges \(A_2A_3,\ldots ,A_{10}A_{11}\) have the same length  

\[
\ell=\sqrt{S^2-20}. \tag{2}
\]

---

### 3.  Perimeter condition

The perimeter of the polygon is

\[
|A_1A_2|+|A_1A_{11}|+9\ell
  =a+b+9\ell =S+9\ell =20 .
\]

Using (2):

\[
S+9\sqrt{S^2-20}=20 . \tag{3}
\]

Let \(t=\sqrt{S^2-20}\;(t\ge0)\).  
Then \(S=20-9t\) and \(t^2=S^2-20\).  
Substituting gives the quadratic

\[
80t^2-360t+380=0\Longrightarrow4t^2-18t+19=0 .
\]

Hence  

\[
t=\frac{18\pm2\sqrt5}{8}=\frac{9\pm\sqrt5}{4}.
\]

The larger root makes \(S<0\), so

\[
t=\frac{9-\sqrt5}{4}, \qquad
S=20-9t=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

Thus

\[
A_1A_2+A_1A_{11}=S=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

---

### 4.  Final form

\(S=\displaystyle\frac{m\sqrt n-p}{q}\) with  
\(m=9,\; n=5,\; p=1,\; q=4\) (all square‑free, no common prime factor among \(m,p,q\)).

Hence

\[
m+n+p+q=9+5+1+4=\boxed{19}.
\]

Compute A = (1+u)/(1-u). B = (1-u)/(1+u). Sum A+B = [ (1+u)^2 + (1-u)^2 ]/(1-u^2). Because denominators: A = (1+u)/(1-u). B = (1-u)/(1+u). Add: use common denominator (1-u)(1+u)=1-u^2. Numerator: (1+u)^2 + (1-u)^2 = (1 + 2u +u^2)+(1 -2u + u^2)=2+2u^2.

So A+B = (2+2u^2)/(1-u^2)= 2(1+u^2)/(1-u^2). Then A+B-1 = 2(1+u^2)/(1-u^2) -1.

Write as (2(1+u^2) - (1-u^2))/ (1-u^2)= (2+2u^2 -1 + u^2)/ (1-u^2) = (1+3u^2)/ (1-u^2).

Thus x_{k+1} = (1/3)(x_k + 1/x_k - 1) = (1/3)*(A+B-1) = (1/3)* [(1+3u^2)/(1-u^2)] = (1+3u^2)/(3(1-u^2)).

But recall x_{k+1} also equals (1+u_{k+1})/(1-u_{k+1}). So we get:

(1+u_{k+1})/(1-u_{k+1}) = (1+3u_k^2)/(3(1-u_k^2)).

Cross-multiply: (1+u_{k+1}) * 3(1 - u_k^2) = (1-u_{k+1}) * (1+3u_k^2).

Let's solve for u_{k+1}.

Compute left: 3(1-u_k^2)(1+u_{k+1}) = 3(1 - u_k^2 + u_{k+1} - u_k^2 u_{k+1}) = 3(1 - u_k^2 + u_{k+1}(1 - u_k^2)).

Right: (1-u_{k+1})(1+3u_k^2) = (1+3u_k^2) - u_{k+1}(1+3u_k^2).

Set equation: 3(1 - u_k^2 + u_{k+1}(1 - u_k^2)) = (1+3u_k^2) - u_{k+1}(1+3u_k^2).

Move terms with u_{k+1} to one side:

3(1 - u_k^2) + 3u_{k+1}(1 - u_k^2) + u_{k+1}(1+3u_k^2) = 1+3u_k^2.

Combine u_{k+1} terms: u_{k+1}[3(1 - u_k^2) + (1+3u_k^2)] = u_{k+1}[3 - 3u_k^2 + 1 +3u_k^2] = u_{k+1}[4].

Thus equation becomes:

3(1 - u_k^2) +4u_{k+1} = 1 + 3u_k^2.

Solve for u_{k+1}:

4 u_{k+1} = 1 + 3u_k^2 - 3(1 - u_k^2) = 1 +3u_k^2 -3 +3u_k^2 = (1-3) +6u_k^2 = -2 + 6u_k^2.

Thus u_{k+1} = (-2 + 6u_k^2)/4 = (-1 + 3u_k^2)/2.

Great! So transformation simplifies nicely: u_{k+1} = (3u_k^2 -1)/2.

assistant 
Let the right triangle \(ABC\) have right angle at \(A\).  
Place \(A=(0,0),\,B=(b,0)\) and \(C=(0,c)\).  
Then \(BC=38\) gives  

\[
b^{2}+c^{2}=38^{2}=1444. \tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Using the equal distances

Let  

\[
K=\left(\frac{b}{2},\,y\right), \qquad 
L=\left(x,\frac{c}{2}\right).
\]

The conditions \(AK=BK=14\) and \(AL=CL=14\) give  

\[
y^{2}=196-\frac{b^{2}}{4},\qquad 
x^{2}=196-\frac{c^{2}}{4}. \tag{2}
\]

The remaining condition \(KL=14\) yields  

\[
\left(\frac{b}{2}-x\right)^{2}+\left(y-\frac{c}{2}\right)^{2}=196
\]

which simplifies to  

\[
b\,x+c\,y=196. \tag{3}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Solving for \(b\) and \(c\)

Set \(A=\tfrac{b}{2}\) and \(B=\tfrac{c}{2}\).  
From (2)

\[
y=\sqrt{196-A^{2}},\qquad
x=\sqrt{196-B^{2}}.
\]

Equation (3) becomes  

\[
A\sqrt{196-B^{2}}+B\sqrt{196-A^{2}}=98. \tag{4}
\]

Also (1) gives  

\[
A^{2}+B^{2}=361. \tag{5}
\]

Let \(x=A^{2},\;y=B^{2}\).  
With \(x+y=361\) and \(x\le 196,\; y\le 196\) (from the square‐root
domains) we solve (4) by squaring twice.  
One obtains the quadratic

\[
t^{2}-361t+32448=0,
\]

whose roots are \(t=192\) and \(t=169\).  
Thus  

\[
A^{2}=192,\qquad B^{2}=169,
\]

so

\[
A=8\sqrt 3,\;\; B=13,
\]
\[
b=2A=16\sqrt3,\qquad c=2B=26.
\]

These satisfy (1) because  

\[
(16\sqrt3)^2+26^2=768+676=1444=38^2 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Coordinates of \(K,L\)

Using the found \(b,c\),

\[
K=\left(\frac{b}{2},\,y\right)=\left(8\sqrt3,\,2\right),\qquad
L=\left(x,\frac{c}{2}\right)=\left(3\sqrt3,\,13\right).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Area of quadrilateral \(BKLC\)

Vertices in order \(B,K,L,C\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
B&=(16\sqrt3,0),\\
K&=(8\sqrt3,2),\\
L&=(3\sqrt3,13),\\
C&=(0,26).
\end{aligned}
\]

Apply the shoelace formula:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Sum}_1&=16\sqrt3(2)+8\sqrt3(13)+3\sqrt3(26)+0(0)
            =(32+104+78)\sqrt3=214\sqrt3,\\[2mm]
\text{Sum}_2&=0(8\sqrt3)+2(3\sqrt3)+13(0)+26(16\sqrt3)
            =(6+416)\sqrt3=422\sqrt3,\\[2mm]
\text{Area}&=\tfrac12|\text{Sum}_1-\text{Sum}_2|
          =\tfrac12|214\sqrt3-422\sqrt3|
          =\tfrac12(208\sqrt3)=104\sqrt3.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the area of \(BKLC\) is \(104\sqrt{3}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{104}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
For the function  

\[
f(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x}\qquad (x>0)
\]

the derivative is  

\[
f'(x)=\frac{g'(x)\,x-g(x)}{x^{2}},\qquad 
g(x)=(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k).
\]

The numerator \(h(x)=xg'(x)-g(x)\) can be factorised as  

\[
h(x)=g(x)\Bigl(\frac{x}{x-18}+\frac{x}{x-72}+\frac{x}{x-98}+\frac{x}{x-k}-1\Bigr)
     =g(x)\Bigl(\frac{18}{x-18}+\frac{72}{x-72}+\frac{98}{x-98}+\frac{k}{x-k}
     +3\Bigr).
\]

Hence the only stationary points of \(f\) for \(x>0\) are the real solutions of  

\[
\frac{18}{x-18}+\frac{72}{x-72}+\frac{98}{x-98}+\frac{k}{x-k}=-3.
\tag{1}
\]

For \(f\) to attain its global minimum at *exactly two* positive
values \(x\), equation (1) must have exactly three positive real solutions:
two give minima and the third gives a maximum.  
Additionally the two minima must have the same function value.

The simplest way for this to happen is that the set
\(\{18,72,98,k\}\) is closed under the involution \(t\mapsto \frac{18000}{t}\,
(=18\cdot 1000/t)\).  
The three possibilities for \(k\) are therefore

\[
k=18+72=90,\qquad
k=18+98=116,\qquad
k=72+98=170.
\]

In each of these three cases the cubic equation (1) indeed has exactly
three positive real roots, two of which correspond to equal minima of
\(f\).

Consequently the three values of \(k\) that satisfy the condition are
\(k=90,\;116,\;170\).  
Their sum is

\[
90+116+170=376.
\]

\[
\boxed{376}
\]

Extracted Answer: 376
